BOROUGH OF WYOMISSING, BERKS COUNTY, PENNSYLVANIA 
ORDINANCE NO. 1465-2026 
 
AN ORDINANCE OF THE BOROUGH OF WYOMISSING, BERKS COUNTY, 
PENNSYLVANIA, AMENDING  THE BOROUGH OF WYOMISSING CODE OF 
ORDINANCES, CHAPTER 27 ENTITLED “ZONING” TO ADD THE DEFINITION OF 
“LANDSCAPE ARCHITECT”, TO IMPOSE UNIFORM, DETAILED REQUIREMENT 
FOR LANDSCAPE PLANS AS PART OF PRELIMINARY AND FINAL LAND 
DEVELOPMENT PLANS, AND  REVISING THE EXISTING LANGUAGE FOR 
UNIFORMITY. 
 
WHEREAS, the Pennsylvania Municipalities Planning Code, act of July 31, 1968, as 
amended, 53 P.S. §§10101 et seq., enables a municipality through its zoning ordinance to regulate 
the uses of property; and 
WHEREAS, pursuant to the Municipalities Planning Code, the Borough of Wyomissing 
enacted the Borough of Wyomissing Zoning Ordinance by Ordinance Number 1234 on February 
11, 2003, which has been amended from time to time and is now known as Borough of 
Wyomissing Zoning Ordinance of 2010, as amended (“Zoning Ordinance”); and 
 WHEREAS, the Zoning Ordinance is codified as Chapter 27 in the Borough of 
Wyomissing Code of Ordinances; and 
 
 WHEREAS, Council for the Borough of Wyomissing desires to amend the Zoning 
Ordinance, Chapter 27 of the Code of Ordinances to provide for detailed landscape plans as part 
of the land development process in the Borough. 
  
NOW THEREFORE BE IT ENACTED and ORDAINED by the Council of the Borough 
of Wyomissing, Berks County, Pennsylvania by the authority of the same as follows: 
 
 SECTION 1.  Amendment to Chapter 27 “Zoning”, Part 2 “Definitions,” Section 201 
“Specific Terms” to add the defined term “Landscape Architect.”  
 
 The Code of the Borough of Wyomissing Chapter 27 entitled “Zoning”, Part 2 entitled 
“Definitions”, Section 201 entitled “Specific Terms” , is hereby amended and supplemented to 
alphabetically add the following term: 
 
 LANDSCAPE ARCHITECT – A landscape architect in the Commonwealth of 
Pennsylvania, with current and active registration. 
 
 SECTION 2.  Amendment to Chapter 27 “Zoning”, Part 6 “General Regulations”, Section 
604 “Landscaping” to add requirements to submit Landscape Plans during land development and 
add regulations regarding Landscape Plans. 
 
DRAFT

2 
 
 The Code of the Borough of Wyomissing Chapter 27 entitled “Zoning”, Part 6 entitled 
“General Regulations”, Section 604 entitled “Landscaping”, is hereby amended and restated in its 
entirety to read as follows: 
 
 §604. Landscaping.  
 
1.  Introduction 
A.  When a Landscape Plan Submission is Required 
(i) A Landscape Plan shall be submitted as part of a preliminary plan or final 
plan (if not preceded by a preliminary plan).  
(ii) The Landscape Plan shall be prepared by a Landscape Architect. 
(iii) When landscape requirements are provided within a specific zoning 
district, the more restrictive landscape requirement shall apply.  
B. What is Required of the Landscape Plan 
(i) The Landscape Plan shall meet the requirements of preliminary and final 
plans as required within the Subdivision and Land Development 
Ordinance.  
(ii) Emphasis shall be placed upon an accurate demonstration of existing 
trees and vegetation to be removed and to remain, as well as, a proposed 
Landscape Plan. 
(iii) Existing and proposed utilities, including site lighting, shall be depicted 
on the Landscape Plan.  
(iv) Any clear sight triangles shall be depicted on the Landscape Plan. 
(v) The Landscape Plan shall clearly indicate what landscape requirements 
the proposed plantings (and existing plantings to remain) are designated 
to, as well as, the calculations utilized to determine the quantity of plants 
within specific requirements. Any landscape requir ement calculations 
that result in a fraction, shall be rounded up to a whole plant.  
(vi) When landscape requirements within a specific zoning district vary from 
this Section, the more restrictive landscape requirement shall apply. 
(vii) Installation details and notes for proposed plants shall be provided. 
 
2. Protection of Existing Trees  
A. What Trees are to be Protected 
(i) Trees located within the public right -of-way shall not be removed or 
disturbed without approval by the Shade Tree Commission. 
(ii) A temporary, sturdy physical barrier, such as a snow fence, shall be 
erected a minimum of one foot outside the dripline or a minimum of 20 
feet from the tree's trunk, whichever is greater, on all sides of freestanding 
trees, tree masses or woodlands prior to major clearing or construction. 
The barrier shall be placed to prevent disturbance to or compaction of soil 
inside the barrier and shall remain until construction is complete. When 
construction is complete, a note on the approved Landscape Plan  shall 
direct the contractor to remove the barrier.  
(iii) Trees to remain which require or incur disturbance during construction, 
will be subject to review by the Borough Arborist to determine if the tree 
DRAFT

3 
 
can be salvaged as a result of this disturbance. A note to this activity shall 
appear on the approved Landscape Plan if work occurs in a public right -
of-way.  
B. Existing Trees to Remain Towards Landscape Requirements  
(i) Existing trees to remain, that are 2 1/2 inches or more in diameter 
(measured at 6” above grade), may be utilized towards respective 
landscape requirements, as may be appropriate. 
(ii) When possible, it is encouraged to preserve and utilize existing 
woodlands and hedges towards landscape requirements for perimeter 
buffers and screens. The quality and density of the vegetation to remain 
will be reviewed upon a case-by-case basis. 
 
3. Required Street Trees 
A. When Street Trees are Required  
(i) Any proposed subdivision or land development shall provide the required 
street trees within twenty feet of the curbed cartway provided there is space 
to accommodate the tree. The Borough is responsible for purchasing and 
installing any required street trees. The developer shall make a payment for 
each required street tree to the Borough, in conjunction with the current fee 
schedule, prior to the release of plans for recording. The developer shall show 
the required street trees on the Landscape Plans to demonstrate coordination 
with the project site.  
(ii) Quantity of Street Trees Required  
a. One s hade tree shall be provided per 45 feet of street frontage which 
demonstrates the open space to accommodate the tree.  
b. An evenly spaced, straight row of trees is preferred to match the existing 
character of Wyomissing Borough. Staggered alignment of trees is 
permitted with approval by the Shade Tree Commission. 
 
4. Required Screen Buffers and Required Site Element Screens 
A. When Screen Buffers are Required 
(i) Along the side and rear yards between residential zoning districts (including 
existing residential uses) and any non-residential zoning district. 
(ii) Along the side and rear yards between residential zoning districts (including 
existing residential uses) and multifamily dwellings, including apartment 
buildings, condominiums, and townhouses. 
(iii) Along the rear yard of any residential reverse frontage lots as required per the 
Subdivision and Land Development Ordinance. 
(iv) Along the front yards of industrial zoning districts and uses. 
B.  When Site Element Screens are Required 
(i) When any of the following items are located within 100 feet of a property line 
or road right-of-way 
a. Dumpsters, trash disposal or recycling areas 
b. Service or loading docks 
c. Truck and trailer parking areas 
d. Outdoor storage 
DRAFT

4 
 
e. Outdoor mechanical yards 
f. Sewage treatment plants and pump stations 
g. Vacuums at car washes 
h. Solar fields 
i. Ground based HVAC units 
j. Ground based water storage elements 
C. The Level of Screening Required by the Screen Buffers and Site Element Screens 
(i) Screen buffers shall consist of a dense mass of trees, shrubs or other plant 
material or fences or walls, or a combination of fences, walls, vegetation and 
landform, extending the full length of the property to serve as a barrier to 
visibility, glare and noise. 
(ii) Screen buffer and site element screens consisting of plantings shall be installed 
at a minimum 6’ height and shall produce within three years a complete, 
continuous visual screen of at least six feet in height. 
(iii)A screen buffer may be substituted with a 6’ height solid screen fence or wall.  
When space allows, additional trees and shrubs shall be included to soften the 
fence or wall, and to buffer views above six feet in height. 
(iv) A solid 6’ height screen fence or wall may be utilized to screen site elements. 
D. Where the Screen Buffer Plantings and Site Element Screens are to be Located 
(i) Screen buffer plantings shall be fully contained between the property lines and 
the yard setback lines. 
(ii) Site element screens may be located outside of the yard setback lines and close 
to the element to be screened. 
 
5. Required Softening Buffers 
A. Purpose of a Softening Buffer 
(i) The p urpose of a softening buffer is to provide additional partial visual 
mitigation and accent while allowing the Landscape Architect flexibility in its 
design. 
(ii) When Softening Buffers are Required 
a. Along all yards of any industrial district or office/research park 
district that are not along residential districts or residential uses. 
b. Along all yards of residential communities consisting of more than 
three dwelling units. 
c. Along the perimeter of stormwater management open basins. 
B. Quantity of Softening Buffer Plantings Required 
(i) 1 Shade tree (minimum 2 1/2” caliper) per 50 linear feet; or 
(ii) 10 Shrubs (minimum 24” height) per 50 linear feet; or 
(iii) 2 Evergreen trees (minimum 6’-8’ height) per 50 linear feet 
C. Where the Softening Buffer Plantings are to be Located 
(i) Softening buffer plantings shall be fully contained between the right-of-way or 
property lines and a point fifteen feet beyond the yard setback lines. 
(ii) The design of a softening buffer is the responsibility of the project landscape 
architect.  A more, naturalistic, clustered grouping of plant material is desired, 
particularly along the perimeter of a stormwater management open basin. 
 
DRAFT

5 
 
6. Required Parking Lot Landscaping 
A. When Parking Lot Landscaping is Required 
(i) For any parking facility with 5 or more parking spaces. 
B. Quantity of Parking Lot Plantings Required 
(i) 1 Deciduous tree for every 10 parking spaces.  Deciduous trees proposed within 
interior parking islands shall be shade trees.  Deciduous trees proposed along 
the perimeter of parking lots may be either shade or ornamental trees. 
(ii) A grouping of shrubs shall be provided along the perimeter of parking facilities 
to prevent headlights from glaring onto adjacent streets and any incompatible 
abutting land uses.  The shrub grouping shall consist of predominantly 
evergreen shrubs or heavily branched deciduous shrubs and shall be designed 
to provide an immediate impact. 
(iii) Shrubs, ornamental grasses, perennials, and groundcover plantings are 
encouraged within interior parking islands, but not required.  Any such 
plantings shall be located so that their mature growth will not impact sight lines 
of parking circulation.  
C. Where the Parking Lot Plantings are to be Located 
(i) Trees shall be planted within landscape parking islands or within 10’ of the 
parking lot perimeter. 
(ii) The center of trees shall be installed minimally 3’ from the edge of curb or 
paving. 
 
7. General Landscape Requirements 
A. Minimum Size of Required Plantings 
(i) Shade trees shall be minimally 2 ½” caliper (measured at 6” above grade) and 
limbed up to 6’ height. 
(ii) Ornamental trees shall be minimally 2 ½” caliper (measured at 6” above grade) 
for single trunk trees and minimally 8’-10’ height for multi-stem trees. 
(iii) Evergreen trees shall be minimally 6’ height. 
(iv)  Shrubs utilized for screening and buffering shall be minimally 2’ height.   
B. Recommended Plant Species 
(i) The species of proposed street trees will be subject to review by the Borough. 
(ii) A minimum of 66% of each plant type (shade tree, ornamental tree, shrub, 
groundcover) shall be of a species native to the northeastern region of the 
United States of America. 
(iii) Any plant species (including cultivars) deemed to be invasive by the 
Commonwealth of Pennsylvania Department of Conservation and Natural 
Resources (PA DCNR) shall be prohibited. 
(iv)  Non-native species are permitted under Item (B)(ii) above, as long as they are 
not invasive. 
(v) Monocultural planting designs shall be avoided.  Biodiversity shall be 
encouraged for range of seasonal interest and protection from disease and pest 
outbreaks. 
C. Required Tree Offsets from Utilities 
(i) The center of any proposed tree shall be offset minimally 10’ from any 
underground or overhead utility. 
DRAFT

6 
 
(ii) All plantings, other than lawn, shall adhere to any restrictions as may be set 
forth by the utility authority. 
 
D. Any plantings proposed within clear sight triangles  shall adhere to the height 
restrictions for clear sight triangles established in this Zoning Ordinance. 
E. All plants shall be in accordance with the latest edition of the American Standard 
for Nursery Stock. 
F. Any landscape area within fifteen feet of a building footprint that is not planted in 
lawn shall contain a natural, fire -retardant, weed deterrent surface.  Fire retardant 
products (natural and manmade) with a maintenance strategy for long term 
fertilization and weed control shall be required. 
G. Guarantee 
(i) All required plantings shall be maintained or replaced permanently. 
(ii) Any plant material which is in decline or has died shall be replaced within six 
months of observance. 
 
8. Recommended Tree Lists:  The following tree species and their cultivars are 
recommended for planting within the Borough of Wyomissing.  Alternate tree species 
may be provided with the approval of the Planning Commission.

BOROUGH OF WYOMISSING, BERKS COUNTY, PENNSYLVANIA 
 
ORDINANCE NO. 1466-2026 
 
AN ORDINANCE OF THE BOROUGH OF WYOMISSING, BERKS COUNTY, 
PENNSYLVANIA, AMENDING THE BOROUGH OF WYOMISSING CODE OF 
ORDINANCES, CHAPTER 22 ENTITLED "SUBDIVISION AND LAND 
DEVELOPMENT", PART 2 ENTITLED "DEFINITIONS", SECTION 204 ENTITLED 
"SPECIFIC TERMS",  TO REVISE THE DEFINITION OF LANDSCAPE ARCHITECT, 
AND TO AMEND PART 4 ENTITLED “PLAN REQUIREMENTS”, SECTION 403 
ENTITLED “PRELIMINARY PLAN”,  PARAGRAPH (1)  TO REQUIRE SUBMISSION 
OF A LANDSCAPE PLAN WITH A PRELIMINARY PLAN, AND TO AMEND PART 4 
ENTITLED “PLAN REQUIREMENTS”, SECTION 404 “FINAL PLAN” TO REQUIRE 
SUBMISSION OF A LANDSCAPE PLAN WITH A FINAL PLAN.  
 
WHEREAS, the Pennsylvania Municipalities Planning Code, A ct of July 31, 1968, as 
amended, 53 P.S. §§10101 et seq ., enables a municipality through its Subdivision and Land 
Development Ordinance to regulate the application process for subdivision and land development; 
and  
 
 WHEREAS, pursuant to the Pennsylvania Municipalities Planning Code  the Borough of 
Wyomissing enacted a Subdivision and Land Development Ordinance by enacting Ordinance 
Number 1125 on October 9, 1990, which has been amended from time to time; and 
 
 WHEREAS, Ordinance Number 1125 was codified in the Code of Ordinances of the 
Borough of Wyomissing as Chapter 22 entitled "Subdivision and Land Development Ordinance" 
(“SALDO”); and 
 
 WHEREAS, the SALDO provides limited information regarding the topic of landscaping in 
land development; and 
 
 WHEREAS, to protect  the welfare of the public and residents of the Borough of 
Wyomissing, and provide for uniform application of the requirement for a landscape plan during 
the land development process, the Council for the Borough of Wyomissing desires to amend and 
supplement the SALDO to require landscape plans as part of both preliminary and final land 
development plans, and to clarify the terms used. 
 
NOW THEREFORE BE IT ENACTED and ORDAINED  by the Council of the Borough 
of Wyomissing, Berks County, Pennsylvania by the authority of the same as follows: 
 
 SECTION 1.  Definition of Landscape Architect.  
 
The Code of the Borough of Wyomissing Chapter 22 entitled "Subdivision and Land 
Development", Part 2 entitled "Definitions ", Section 204 entitled " Specific Terms ", term 
“Landscape Architect” is amended and restated to read in its entirety to read as follows:  
 
DRAFT

2 
 
§204. Definitions. 
 
 LANDSCAPE ARCHITECT – a landscape architect in the Commonwealth of 
Pennsylvania with current and active registration.  
 
 SECTION 2. Submission of Landscape Plan with Preliminary Plan.  
 
The Code of the Borough of Wyomissing Chapter 22 entitled "Subdivision and Land 
Development", Part 4 entitled "Plan Requirements ", Section 403 entitled "Preliminary Plan ", 
Paragraph 1(P) is amended and supplemented to read as follows:  
 
(10) A landscape plan shall be submitted per the requirements of Chapter 27, 
Zoning Ordinance. 
 
 SECTION 3. Submission of Landscape Plan with Final Plan. 
 
The Code of the Borough of Wyomissing Chapter 22 entitled "Subdivision and Land 
Development", Part 4 entitled "Plan Requirements", Section 404 entitled "Final Plan", Paragraph 
6 is amended and restated in its entirety to read as follows:  
 
A landscape plan shall be submitted per the requirements of Chapter 27, Zoning 
Ordinance. 
 
 SECTION 4.    Repeal of Ordinances . 
 
Any ordinance or part of ordinances conflicting with the provisions of this Ordinance are 
hereby repealed insofar as they are inconsistent with this Ordinance’s provision.   
 
SECTION 5.    Severability . 
 
 If any article, section, subsection, provision, regulation, limitation, restriction, sentence, 
clause, phrase or word in this Ordinance, is, for any reason declared to be illegal, unconstitutional 
or invalid, by any Court of competent jurisdiction, this de cision shall not affect or impair the 
validity of the Ordinance as a whole, or any other article, section, subsection, provision, regulation, 
limitation, restriction, sentence, clause, phrase, word, or remaining portion of the within 
Ordinance.  The Borough Council of the Borough of Wyomissing, Pennsylvania, hereby declares 
that it would have adopted the within Ordinance and each article, section, subsection, provision, 
regulation, limitation, restriction, sentence, clause, phrase and word thereof, irrespective of the 
limitations, restrictions, sentences, clauses, phrases, or word that may be declared illegal, 
unconstitutional or invalid. 
 
SECTION 4.   Effective Date.   
 
 This Ordinance shall become effective at the earliest date permitted by law. 
 . 
 
DRAFT

3 
 
SECTION 5.    Code of Ordinances. 
 
 The Code of Ordinances, as amended, of the Borough of Wyomissing, Berks County, 
Pennsylvania shall be and remain unchanged and in full force and effect except as amended, 
supplemented, and modified by this Ordinance.  This Ordinance shall become a part of  this Code 
of Ordinances upon adoption.

BOROUGH OF WYOMISSING – COUNCIL MINUTES 
OCTOBER 13, 2026 – 7:00 P.M. 
 
The Borough of Wyomissing Council held their regular meeting in the Borough of Wyomissing 
Council Chambers on the above date and time. Council President Kristen M. Gajewski called the 
meeting to order at 7:00 p.m. with the following persons present: Jennifer L. Reimer, Richard A. 
Weinhoffer, Stephen D. Brunner, Jana R. Barnett, Christopher F. Valente, and Adam C. Berkeley; 
Mayor Frederick C. Levering, Treasurer David Reeser, Borough Solicitor Heidi Masano, Interim 
Borough Manager Eden Ratliff, Borough Engineer Representative Nick Perilli, Public Works Director 
James Babb, Police Chief John Phillips, Fire Chief Eric Needle, Interim Chief Financial Officer Scott 
Boehret, and Borough Secretary Melissa Miller.  
CALL TO ORDER 
 
PLEDGE OF ALLEGIANCE 
ROLL CALL 
VISITORS: 
Tanya Grilli – 1805 Squire Court 
Carol Bausher – 1542 Dauphin Avenue 
Tom Bausher – 1542 Dauphin Avenue

David Walker – 1039 Terrace Avenue/ Wyomissing Library 
Adrian Jadic – 48 Cardinal Road 
 
APPROVAL OF AGENDA: 
Councilmember Barnett made a motion, seconded by Councilmember Brunner to approve the 
consent agenda as presented, all in favor, 7-0. 
 
Interim Borough Manager Eden Ratliff shared with Council that the Planning Commission met and 
approved recommending to Council, the Conditional Use for 1714 State Hill Road, with 
considerations set forth by the Commission. He noted the proposed use is consistent with the 
overlay district. A formal memo will be provided to Council, and date for the hearing established. 
PUBLIC COMMENTS (agenda items): 
Dr. Angela Adler, 1544 Cleveland Avenue provided public comment, thanking Council for broad 
sights as addressing various challenges, and provided a budgetary suggestion relative to the Public 
Works facility and implications.  
Adrian Jadic, 48 Cardinal Road, provided public comment relative to the Borough’s public 
comment session location on the agenda, the Borough’s interim manager, lack of evening 
committee meetings, requests for proposals, and disposing of documents.  
DRAFT

Council President Gajewski reminded the public that the former manager resigned and was not 
fired, and that Council has the responsibility to make decisions to benefit all residents in the 
Borough. 
CONSENT AGENDA: 
The following consent agenda items were motioned for approval by Councilmember Barnett, 
seconded by Councilmember Berkeley, all in favor, 7-0: 
a. Approval of Council Minutes –September 16, 2026 Council meeting and 
September 30, 2026 Special Council meeting 
b. Approval of Payment Application No. 1 from Reamstown Excavating, Inc., in 
the amount of $101,749.75 for 2026 50/50 Sidewalk work 
c. Acceptance of the bid and award the 2027, one-year mowing contract to Crouse 
Construction LLC., in the amount of $90,125, as the lowest responsible bid and, 
to the extent that Crouse’s bid security amount is less than 10% of the bid, that 
potential defect is waived because the amount does otherwise comply with the 
bid security amount required in the bid specs and the Borough reserved the 
right to waive defects and informalities in the bid documents 
d. Approval of the quote received from Jeremiah Wright Masonry for $13,100 for 
the damaged Wyomissing Hills pillars 
e. Approval of the towing list for the Police Department, encompassing Spitler 
Towing as the primary tow company, and V&M Towing as the secondary tow 
company 
f. Adoption of Resolution No. 2026-28 as to disposition of accounts receivable 
files and ledgers which have been retained longer than 7 years 
g. Adoption of Resolution No. 2026-29 as to disposition of accounts payable files 
and ledgers which have been retained longer than 7 years 
h. Adoption of Resolution No. 2026-30 as to application of the Category 4 Local 
Share Account in the amount of $80,000 for Fire Department renovations 
i. Authorization of the first amendment to the agreement with CG Broadcasting 
Developer, LLC., for the developer to install an upgraded sanitary sewer line as 
part of Spring Township project, in the vicinity of a Borough-owned water 
main within the US 222 Limited Access Utility Easement, abiding by 
agreement terms established within, if modifications are required for such 
installation, and extending the completion deadline to no later than June 30, 
2027

of Recreation 
 
It was pointed out that the document destruction Resolutions are pertinent to financial documents 
exceeding seven years old, that are permitted to be destroyed under the rules set forth by the 
Pennsylvania Historical and Museum Commission. 
 
 
DRAFT

ACTION ITEMS: 
No items were presented to Council for action. 
 
MAYOR’S REPORT: 
Mayor Frederick Levering noted that he had no report highlights for the month. He did 
congratulate the Wyomissing School District for ranking in the top 2,000 in the nation, top 80 
in the state, and top 2 in the County. He also spoke to Council about the Stoltzfus Homestead 
along Tulpehocken Road, ultimately reminding all to not lose sight of history and traditions.  
 
Mayor Levering also questioned the grant received for the streambank restoration and 
parkland trails, and it was pointed out this was relevant to work set forth in the parkland and 
forestry study. There was also an area discussed for controlling invasive species and 
incorporate deer management.  
 
STAFF REPORTS: 
The Library, Police, Fire, Public Works/Engineering, and Tax Collector’s reports were 
included in the packet for review.  
 
INTERIM BOROUGH MANAGER & INTERIM FINANCE DIRECTOR REPORT: 
Interim Borough Manager Ratliff shared with Council that the proposed overlay district was 
sent to the Berks County Planning Commission, with no comments received. This Ordinance 
should be on course to have the public hearing during the November Council meeting. He also 
pointed out there are no updates on 1714 State Hill Road, 915 Penn Avenue, or the Crown 
Plaza. Interim Borough Manager Ratliff also reminded Council of Spooky Hollow event being 
held on October 17, 2026 at 2:00 pm.   
 
2027 BUDGET DEVELOPMENT: 
Interim Borough Manager Ratliff first shared the 2027 budget preparation schedule with the 
public. 
 
During the presentation, there was discussion about the restructuring of the general fund, 
leaving a deficit of $2.4 to $2.7 million, and the fact that some of the enterprise funds were 
drawn on to pay the deficit, of which there are specific regulations that must be followed when 
moving funds as overhead transfers. It was noted that in 2026, there were $750,000 in capital 
projects, and the Borough was only responsible for paying $300,000. It was noted that the 
Borough is financially healthy, and wise financial decisions were made previously. Staff will 
continue to review the cost of operations, debt service arrangements, capital contributions, and 
contributions to reserve accounts.  
 
As per the presentation, the 2027 preliminary general fund revenues are $14,442,200, 
expenditures are $20,517,601, with a negative net of $6,075,401. 
 
The presentation further reviewed operational drivers including bargaining, other wages, 
health, general insurance, and overall inflation. This accounts for approximately $800,000 in 
2027. To impact the budget, the Borough would have to decrease service levels. 
 
DRAFT

For the library program, the proposed expenditure is $254,253, of which the Borough funds a 
portion of the library.  
 
Summer Playground revenues were $75,692 expenditures were $106,762, leaving a negative 
net of $31,070. There was much discussion around the program offering including fees, 
expanding the program times, timeline, and ages, inclusion of West Reading youth, as well as 
how much tax burden should be placed on residents for the program.  
 
Similarly, the pool was discussed next, with revenues of $156,550, expenditures of $220,019, 
leaving a negative net of $63,469. It was noted that major capital work is needed, as well as an 
overall needs assessment conducted. There was discussion of the efforts by the pool 
subcommittee to increase awareness of the pool and activities, West Reading youth possibly 
joining the Wyomissing swim team, and the need to review staffing levels. 
 
The presentation also touched base on code enforcement services, noting that all are 
outsourced at this time due to staff changes. The cost is $109,000 to the Borough, with an 
aspect of customer service that is lacking. The Borough currently participates in proactive 
code enforcement 3 times per week, but has reviewed switching to reactive enforcement. It 
was pointed out that most municipalities are using a reactive enforcement approach. If the 
aspects completed by the third-party services are brought in-house, the Borough would break 
even and improve customer service. It was discussed that building inspections would still be 
outsourced due to the lack of volume with inspections. It was pointed out that planning, 
zoning, and permitting would need to be fulfilled by two full-time people at the market rate 
established in Berks County.   
 
There was discussion that it was difficult to consider raising taxes years ago when the Borough 
had a significant fund balance, and the now declining commercial locations, were thriving.  
 
The general fund beginning in 2026 was $3.4 million with 20% available for operating costs. 
In December 2026, the general fund will decrease to $3.04 million with 18.4% available for 
operating costs, with the same general fund balance to begin 2027. It is estimated that the 
ending general fund balance for December 2027 will be negative $3.036 million, with -18% 
available for operating costs. At the next Council meeting, three options will be brought to the 
table for discussion, about next steps moving forward. There was also brief discussion about 
restricting the chart of accounts to better align expenditures with their appropriate 
classifications. A tax bill sample was shown to the public for better understanding of the 
taxation obtained by the Borough.  
 
The public was reminded of the public feedback forum available, as well as following 
budgeting progress on the Borough’s website.  
 
PUBLIC COMMENT (agenda or non-agenda items): 
Adrian Jadic, 48 Cardinal Road provided public comment during the codes enforcement 
portion of the discussion pertaining to having more than one company to select from for 
inspections. During the end public comment session, he provided public comment on the 
document destruction process, codes service and RTK requests, public comment available on 
DRAFT

the agenda after the agenda is voted on, and taxation. 
 
Council President Gajewski indicated her comments were not specifically directed at one 
resident.  
 
Tom Bausher, 1542 Dauphin Avenue provided public comment during the codes enforcement 
portion of the discussion pertaining to fire inspections.  
 
Tanya Grilli,1805 Squire Court provided public comment during the general fund and tax 
discussion pertaining to the fire tax fund classification.  
 
David Reeser, 1110 Fairview Avenue provided public comment about the old Council 
Chambers table. 
 
COUNCIL COMMENT: 
Council members collectively thanked the residents and staff for budget participation and 
preparation. Councilmember Barnett questioned the status of the Refuse and Recycling 
environmental advisory committee, and reiterated that many residents are willing to participate 
on such committee, and also questioned the status of the Data Center Ordinance. Council 
President Gajewski prepared and read written comment.   
 
 
The Wyomissing Borough Council meeting adjourned at 9:02 p.m.

Written Public Comments
October 13, 2026 Borough Council meeting
Adrian Jadic | Wyomissing resident
Prepared 2026-10-19 21:03:05 EDT (UTC-04:00; America/New_York)
To Melissa Miller, Borough Secretary, and Wyomissing Borough Council:
Please add these expanded written comments to the October 13 meeting record and publish
them with the Council materials. The recording remains the record of my oral remarks. Updates
after the meeting are dated below.
1. Give public proposals a decision.
When a resident makes a practical proposal, say what happens next: a committee referral, a
vote, a decision date, or a reason for rejecting it. Residents should not have to return month
after month for an answer.
I asked for evening committee meetings on January 13. Nine months later, committees still
meet during the working day. Put the remaining daytime meetings to a Council vote. If a
committee needs a different arrangement, explain why. Council and Recreation already meet
in the evening. [1]
The Council president clarified that her response concerned my broader criticism of Council,
not specifically records preservation. That clarification belongs in the record. My concern
remains: critical comments deserve the same respectful consideration as favorable ones. A
resident does not need to speak for a majority to raise a valid question. [5]
Adopting the agenda did not approve the consent items. Before the consent vote, make clear
that residents may ask for separate consideration and Council may grant it. Give contested
matters their own discussion and decision. [5]
2. Set goals and a timetable for interim management.
Publish the interim assignment's goals, responsible people, deadlines, results and expected
remaining cost. Include the permanent-manager recruitment timetable. Report progress
publicly and explain delays.
Cumru appointed an interim manager and began a permanent-manager search in January. It
appointed a permanent manager in September. Its refuse procurement drew several
companies and included another bidding round. What will Wyomissing learn from that process?
Show residents the timetable and results here. [2]
3. Show taxpayers what they are getting for their money.
The September 16 draft minutes identify purchasing reform as a priority. Publish the proposed
rules, competition requirements and adoption date. [3]
The circle-wall records show Spayd's earlier $28,500 proposal and New Castle's $41,613 formal
bid. Explain the scope and price differences, and why proceeding with the single formal bid
offered good value. The price difference alone does not establish a saving. [4]
OCTOBER 13 COUNCIL | WRITTEN SUPPLEMENT
Adrian Jadic | Prepared October 19, 2026 1 / 3
DRAFT

The towing packet contains proposals and rates. I found no explanation there for the primary
and secondary designations. Publish the comparison: total charges for equivalent tows,
response times, coverage standards, applicable rates and current insurance. Calling towing a
professional service does not explain the choice. [3]
The packet records a $13,200 Kate & Co retainer through Stonehill Ratliff. What period does it
cover? What work was promised and completed? Explain how it supplements staff duties and
how Council measures its value. [3]
4. Preserve financial records before they are lost.
Council approved Resolutions 2026-28 and 2026-29 on October 13. That does not establish
that records have been destroyed. Defer disposal while the Borough documents what would be
lost, what survives and the preservation options. [3, 5]
The briefing anticipates no significant financial impact but supplies no inventory of the actual
files. Publish a file inventory and date ranges, who reviewed the records, the criteria used, and
how records requiring longer retention or having historical value will be protected. [3]
A check register can show who was paid and how much. Invoices and vouchers can show what
was purchased and why payment was approved. Identify the surviving records that preserve
that detail and explain how residents can obtain them.
Show the staff time and cost of disposal compared with preservation. Seek archival advice and
assess scanning and grant options. My no-charge offer remains available for an agreed batch,
indexed scans and return of originals under Borough-approved terms. Any plan must account
for the Borough's staff work.
As of October 19, the Borough has acknowledged my October 12 records request and given
November 18 as its response target. That does not confirm a preservation hold. Confirm in
writing how responsive records are being protected and which custodians were notified. My
existing request remains unchanged. [6]
5. Make code enforcement fair and open to scrutiny.
Judge code enforcement by consistent treatment, response, cost and accountability, whether
the work is done by employees or contractors.
Publish enforcement policies, service standards, complaint and appeal procedures, and reports
on notices and outcomes. Release records where lawful, protect private information, and
explain specific grounds for withholding records. Residents need a way to check whether
comparable cases receive comparable treatment.
6. Show the full budget costs and test alternatives.
Before asking households to pay more, separate recurring operations from grants, transfers,
capital, debt and other one-time items. Show the cost of current services, proposed changes
and outside contracts, including fees and transition costs. Do not present a year-to-date result
as the recurring operating position.
Compare taxes for the same year and on the same basis. Include separately imposed municipal
levies and explain whether fire and EMS are covered. Show mandatory service charges
separately. General-purpose millage alone does not show a household's full burden.
OCTOBER 13 COUNCIL | WRITTEN SUPPLEMENT
Adrian Jadic | Prepared October 19, 2026 2 / 3
DRAFT

Ask neighboring municipalities, including West Reading, about joint purchasing, shared
equipment, coordinated capital plans and service agreements. Assess police and fire
cooperation against coverage, staffing, governance, labor obligations and transition costs.
Publish the costs and service effects before deciding.
The October 13 timetable lists the Unmet Needs Report for October 21 and the decision
hearing for October 28. In the public sources checked on October 19, I did not locate the report,
and the hearing time remained TBA. Publish the report link and hearing time, allowing
residents time to review the material before decisions. [7]
Please respond in writing with the requests Council will take up, who is responsible and the
expected decision dates. We are paying for change. Show the results and put dates on the
work that remains.

BOROUGH OF WYOMISSING – SPECIAL COUNCIL MINUTES 
OCTOBER 28, 2026 – 7:00 P.M. 
 
The Borough of Wyomissing Council held their special budget meeting in the Borough of Wyomissing 
Council Chambers on the above date and time. Council President Kristen M. Gajewski called the 
meeting to order at 7:00 p.m. with the following persons present: Jennifer L. Reimer, Richard A. 
Weinhoffer, Stephen D. Brunner, Jana R. Barnett, Christopher F. Valente (7:05 pm), and Adam C. 
Berkeley (left at 7:19 pm); Mayor Frederick C. Levering, Treasurer David Reeser, Public Works 
Director James Babb, Police Chief John Phillips, Fire Chief Eric Needle, Interim Borough Manager 
Eden Ratliff, Interim Chief Financial Officer Scott Boehret, and Borough Secretary Melissa Miller. The 
following persons were absent: Borough Solicitor Heidi Masano and Borough Engineer Jim McCarthy. 
CALL TO ORDER 
 
PLEDGE OF ALLEGIANCE 
ROLL CALL 
VISITORS: 
Carol Bausher – 1542 Dauphin Avenue 
Tom Bausher – 1542 Dauphin Avenue 
Tim McDonough – 21 Hummingbird Road 
Dan Macrina – 306 Evans Avenue 
Barbara Reeser – 1110 Fairview Avenue 
David Walker – 1039 Terrace Avenue/ Wyomissing Library 
Adrian Jadic – 48 Cardinal Road 
Melissa Phillips – 1942 Squire Court 
Joseph Neidert – Kraft Municipal Group 
Tony Tucci – Western Berks Ambulance Association 
 
PUBLIC COMMENTS (agenda items): 
Adrian Jadic, 48 Cardinal Road, provided public comment relative to tax increase opposition, 
deliverables and deadlines for consultants, debt service and borrowing decisions, the proposed 
$10 million budget increase for 2027, and better planning for value and cost. Later in the 
meeting he provided public comment about consultants not being on a limited contract with 
deadlines to finish specific tasks in a specific time, increasing costs, the apparent lack of public 
trust, and why is an Interim Manager being allowed to destroy records. 
 
Tim McDonough, 21 Hummingbird Road, provided public comment relative to revenue 
opportunities with economic development, various expense issues with the proposed budget, and 
making efforts to make cuts in the budget. Later in the meeting, he questioned possibilities with the 
Borough-owned Huyett House, questioned the Berkshire Mall tax base, and questioned the 
proposed addition of 5 people and proposed tax increase.  
DRAFT

Tom Bausher, 1542 Dauphin Avenue, provided public comment relative to hiring a permanent 
Borough Manager and Business Manager, the Relief Association’s prior payment of the County 
dispatch fee, funding EMS, decision on how and which portions of the Borough pay for JMA, 
Spring Township, and Reading sewer portions, and concerns about the proposal for three 
administrative salaries for the fire department.  
Dan Macrina, 306 Evans Avenue, provided public comment later in the meeting, pertaining to the 
length of time the Interim Borough Manager has been at the Borough, the amount of work staff are 
currently taking on, the need to fix the culture based on past experiences, and the low salaries 
proposed for the proposed new administrative level personnel. Later in the meeting he questioned 
the long-term impacts of cutting services. 
Carol Bausher, 1542 Dauphin Avenue, provided public comment later in the meeting, pertaining to 
the budget forum sheet, and not everyone knowing about it.  
David Reeser, 1110 Fairview Avenue, provided public comment later in the meeting, pertaining to 
the use of the Communications team.  
2027 BUDGET DEVELOPMENT: 
Interim Borough Manager Ratliff first thanked department heads, Kate & Co., and Transcend 
Finance for their efforts on the 2027 budget, and further shared the 2027 budget preparation 
schedule with the public. He reminded the public that the unmet needs report was posted on 
the budget portion of the Borough’s website on October 21, 2026, and can be accessed by also 
using the provided QR code. Upon the deadline being reached for the community feedback 
form to be completed, there were 29 responses relative to balancing the budget, no tax 
increases, lessen the use of outside consultants, refuse and recycling concerns, and streets, 
traffic, and safety being number one on the concerns. He reminded the public that the 2026 
general fund was restructured and since refined to have a $2.2 million deficit.  
 
Reviewing the 2026 revenue projections, the 2026 budget was $16,005,254, and it is 
anticipated that the Borough will exceed budget by $499,446, and end at $16,504,700. It was 
pointed out that real estate transfers, business privilege tax, and the health care reimbursement 
are anticipated to fall under budget respectively at $213,000, $160,000, and $153,000. The 
earned income tax is however, anticipated to be higher than budgeted. It was noted that the 
Borough’s Public Works switch from Borough insurance to the Teamsters plan, may have 
impacted the health fund reimbursement rate.  
 
Interim Manager Ratliff identified various drivers impacting budget including wages, salaries, 
health, pension, payroll tax, other benefits, liability and workers compensation. In many cases, 
these items are established in the collective bargaining agreements, and he noted that annual 
growth is between 4.5% to 5.3%, or $800,000 for 2027.  
 
Reviewing the preliminary general fund, revenues are anticipated to be $14,442,200, with 
expenditures of $20,517,601, leaving a negative net position of $6,075,401. He reminded the 
public that not all 2027 budgetary expenditures will be funded, this was just an idea of the 
impact if all were to be funded. When looking at the 2027 revised position, revenues are 
anticipated to be $14,577,100, with expenditures of $18,192,284, leaving a negative net 
position of $3,615,184. It was noted that the general fund has been overspending and cannot 
DRAFT

continue to use the fund balance to cover expenditures. Going into 2027, a deficit will exist, 
and the fund balance will not be able to cover it. A slide was shown of the 10-year fund 
balance status and projection, and the noted importance of looking at the financial health for 
the future.  
 
Interim Manager Ratliff indicated that the 2027 budget scenarios were made available on the 
Borough’s website, and he started further discussions of staffing needs. He identified the 
proposed addition of a full-time Administrative Assistant with a salary of $55,000 to $60,000 
per year, and with benefits included, $115,000. A full-time Zoning Officer and Codes 
Enforcement position was proposed for a salary of $70,000 to $80,000 per year, and including 
benefits, $144,000. A full-time Permit Coordinator was proposed with a salary of $55,000 to 
$60,000 per year, and with benefits included, $115,000. A full-time Accounts Receivable 
Coordinator was proposed with a salary of $55,000 to $60,000 per year, and with benefits 
included, $115,000. Lastly, a full-time Deputy Fire Chief was proposed with a salary of 
$102,000 to $107,000 per year, and with benefits included, $176,000.  
 
There was much discussion and comment about the inclusion of staff, and it was pointed out 
that internal controls and segregation of duties is needed, especially in the financial capacity to 
safeguard the Borough and staff as a preventative measure for fraudulent activity. It was 
pointed out that a segregation of duties may not be feasible, rather reviews could be conducted. 
Interim Chief Financial Officer Scott Boehret shared that the inclusion of the Accounts 
Receivable position is an effort for succession planning.  
 
There was further discussion on the hiring of a Finance Manager, and questions as to how 
many days per week the current Interim Manager and Interim CFO are working. While it was 
noted that it depends on the week for the Interim CFO, the Interim Manager is 5 days a week 
which is also dependent on the week. It was pointed out that staff are at their maximum 
capacity, and there was also discussion about the proposed Deputy Fire Chief position.  
 
When reviewing the organizational chart, Interim Manager Ratliff shared that while five new 
positions are proposed, two positions including functions of Zonings, Codes, and Permitting 
will break even of which services are currently outsourced to a third party vendor. The public 
was also reminded that certain aspects of McCarthy Engineering can be rebilled, of which the 
Borough is still responsible for $265,000 for services yearly.  
 
The slide for 2027 consultant expenditures was reviewed. There was much discussion about 
operational expectations identified by Council including operational improvements, improving 
policy, governance, and overall helping Council rebuild. Discussion also ensued about the 
consultant rates, with Council indicating that if a full-time Borough Manager were hired, 
market rate is approximately $185,000 plus 68% benefits costs, with an overall Manager cost 
of $320,000 to $330,000 per year, slightly lower than the Interim Borough Manager rates. 
 
Questions arose as to the contract lengths, and if the Communications team was worth the 
expense. It was noted that the Interim Borough Manager and Interim CFO were working 
month to month, and Council President Gajewski noted that the Communications team is 
assisting with information preparation and dissemination to the public, making the process 
DRAFT

easier than last year to follow. It was also pointed out that public expects information to be 
transparent and include the public to make a meaningful impact. There was also question as to 
why the Communications team wasn’t kept local, and it was noted that could be looked at in 
the future. Councilmember Reimer indicated that a Communications person could be hired on 
staff for $75,000, but the inclusion of benefits did not make the rate much different than using 
a consultant. It was also noted that an intern could assist with communications, and there was 
also concern of the 10% upcharge by Stonehill Ratliff for the communications services. Later 
in the meeting, Councilmember Brunner expressed concerns with the rate for the 
Communications team.   
 
Councilmember Valente indicated that Council was aware that more would be paid for the 
consultant services, and Council’s expectation was that Eden, Jeffrey, and Scott would help 
move Council into a different direction. He again reiterated the cost difference of a full-time 
staff person versus the consultants, acknowledging the difference, but feels the Borough can 
handle the difference. Councilmember Reimer also pointed out that other internal items were 
found that need correction, thus the longer tenure at the Borough for the consultants. Council 
has a goal of finding a quality manager, and one who is able to walk into a more solid 
structure.  
 
Councilmember Reimer indicated that Council is unsure when the tenure of the consultants 
will end, but certain aspects need to be revised within the Borough to attract a quality 
manager.  
 
The public meeting recessed from 8:19 pm to 8:26 pm. 
 
Interim Manager Ratliff reviewed the spending analysis process with fixed versus 
discretionary spending. He pointed out the issue is not necessarily with spending, it’s the 
decreased revenue. This led to a policy level decision about services.  
 
He then began reviewing the funding scenarios, noting that if the Borough would pay for all of 
the proposed capital projects, taxes would need to be raised 7.75 mils. If services were to be 
cut 0 mils would be needed. Other scenarios include a 3 mil tax increase, the inclusion of debt 
service, and some utilization of reserve funds, or a 3 mil tax increase and no capital projects, 
or a 4 mil tax increase, less utilization of reserve funds, and less debt service. Interim Manager 
Ratliff pointed out that scenarios 1, 2, and 5 are not realistic according to Council, and he also 
pointed out the lack of fund balance policy.  
 
Scenario 3 would impose a 3 mil tax increase, have a $2.5 million bond, have $410,000 in debt 
service, and pay for some operations out of the existing fund balance. Reserves as a percentage 
of spending would be 9.8% with a 2027 general fund balance of $1,805,715. Bond issuance 
would need a capital plan, of which will require work on the Borough’s part to develop such. 
Interim Manager Ratliff foresees the need for a 5-year policy document to guide Council of 
when capital funding is needed. It was again reiterated that overtaxing is not necessarily 
needed now, when the cost of such project can span into future years as to the cost being 
spread over various generations.  
 
DRAFT

Scenario 4 would impose a 4 mil tax increase, require less debt, and paying some capital prior 
to bond issuance. If a later project is proposed, some cash can be held for such. The cost to 
borrow on a $2.5 million bond is $410,000. Bank financing can also be evaluated, but that 
operates on a day to day basis. This would leave 14.3% in reserves and will help during 2028 
budget preparation.  
 
The components of Wyomissing’s tax bill were re-reviwed as were the median home values in 
the various sections of the Borough. It was reiterated that median was being used for the 
discussion, not average as was previously misstated. The County comparison ranking puts 
Wyomissing at 23 of 72 with the current real estate mils, and within the Borough 
classification, 19 of 27. A 3 mil increase is 45% in the resident’s Borough tax liability, and a 4 
mil increase is 61% in the resident’s Borough tax liability. It was pointed out that if taxes are 
not increased, than services and employees need to be cut.  
 
There was again much concern that implementing a tax increase is unruly unless Council 
makes an effort to cut expenditures, not necessarily people. It was again pointed out that 2025 
versus 2026 budget line items will not be an even comparison since the 2026 budget was 
reclassified in preparation for 2027 budgeting. A few restructured item categories, were 
discussed.  
 
Several services were discussed, and the hypothetical question posed if the community would 
like for those services to be discontinued or lessened; leaf collection, July 4th parade, etc. 
Again, comment was made that the Borough has a revenue issue.  
 
Councilmember Brunner pointed out the larger commercial parcels in the Borough with 
significantly decreased tax bases, causing a drastic decline in revenue. He also noted that 
recreation makes a difference in the community, police and fire are working well despite his 
continued concerns of the Borough’s Fire Department going into other municipalities. On the 
other hand, he noted that having the Fire Department helps Borough property owners with 
their property insurance. He explained that general operations are becoming more expensive as 
everyone is seeing inflation, and there was further discussion and clarification on the water, 
sewer, and refuse overhead transfers.  
 
Mayor Levering commented that while Wyomissing’s Fire Department is going to other 
municipalities, the departments they are assisting, will often come to Wyomissing as mutual 
aid. Relative to economic development, he feels the modifications to the overlay district 
coming before Council in November, will incentivize development and motivate development 
of surrounding areas.  
 
Council began discussions of the scenarios at hand with Council President Gajewski in favor 
of Scenario 3 or 4, pointing out that while it is not ideal to have another tax increase for 2028, 
she would feel comfortable with Scenario 3. She feels there are opportunities to save money, 
insource some items, and utilize purchase orders.  
 
Councilmember Brunner indicated he is comfortable with Scenario 3, and working towards 
making expenditure cuts from the 2027 request list. He questioned if the $125,000 
DRAFT

architectural rendering fee at Borough Hall is necessary in 2027, as the Borough does not have 
the $2 million matching funds to complete the project. He pointed out that he does not want to 
cut services, nor decrease services either.  
 
Councilmember Reimer pointed out that a 3 mil increase for the average household was $130 
or $3 per week. There was discussion about looking at program reduction in the aspect of 
recreation, or look at not holding a specific event if there is no sponsor. Interim Manager 
Ratliff indicated there is currently no sponsorship policy in place.  
 
Interim Manager Ratliff shared that the goal of this meeting was to obtain feedback and 
guidance, and to provide transparency and education. 
 
Councilmember Brunner again reiterated he is okay with the tax increase, however department 
heads must make an effort to reduce expenditures by at least $300,000. Councilmember 
Barnett was in favor of a 4 mil increase, was okay with the 3 mil increase, but either way, 
concurred with Councilmember Brunner that spending cuts are needed. He recommended 
possibly cutting the communications budget to $125,000. Councilmember Valente also 
concurred with making efforts to be fiscally responsible and make spending cuts.  
 
Interim Manager Ratliff reiterated that Council has advised Borough administration to make 
$250,000 in operation cuts prior to reconvening.  
 
Council further discussed that staff hiring will be delayed until the process is complete, which 
holds true for the Deputy Fire Chief position, but it was noted that a full year salary and 
benefits was included in the budget process so Council could understand the impact moving 
forward. There was discussion about not delaying the Zoning and Codes related positions if it 
is wash from the amount currently being spent.  
 
Based on Council’s directive to identify operational spending cuts, Councilmember Reimer 
made a motion, seconded by Councilmember Barnett to reconvene the budget meeting on 
Tuesday, November 3, 2026 at 7:00 pm in Council Chambers, all in favor, 6-0. 
 
PUBLIC COMMENT (agenda or non-agenda items): 
This will be addressed at the reconvened budget meeting on November 3, 2026. 
 
COUNCIL COMMENT: 
This will be addressed at the reconvened budget meeting on November 3, 2026.

Adrian Jadic | October 28, 2026 Council budget meeting 1
Written comments following the October 28, 2026
budget meeting
Adrian Jadic | Prepared October 30, 2026
This is a later edited written version of my oral comments, with the clarifications below. It is not a
verbatim transcript.
I oppose the tax increases presented at the meeting. Council should first bring residents a
lower-spending budget. Zero additional mills deserves serious consideration: turn the
smaller-Borough scenario into specific cuts, annual net savings and service choices.
A smaller immediate tax increase supported by borrowing is not, by itself, a spending cut. Show
the full cash-versus-borrowing costs of the same projects, including annual repayments and
total cost over the loan term. Explain how debt service would be funded and whether future tax
increases would be needed. Council must own the long-term borrowing decision.
The preliminary 2027 budget proposes $600,000 across the identified management and
finance contract lines, plus $160,000 for communications, marketing and public relations.
These are proposed allocations. What measurable recurring savings has management
delivered, and what targets and deadlines has Council set?
When does interim management end, and what is the permanent staffing plan? I want a
defined scope, deliverables and an end point for interim work. I also questioned the value of
communications spending and asked for concrete evidence of its output, with consideration of
interns or other lower-cost approaches.
I objected to having interim management make records-destruction decisions and asked that
those decisions be left to the permanent manager.
I asked that the presentation distinguish average from median and use the terms accurately.
Eden acknowledged that the slide said median and that this was the word he should have used.
Residents need clearly identified statistical measures to understand the proposed tax impact.
Higher property taxes make homeownership more expensive and could make Wyomissing less
attractive to buyers. Telling residents we need more money is easy. We need a plan for better
value and lower costs. Review staffing, contracts, program subsidies and procurement options,
and show the costs, net annual savings and service effects before asking residents to pay
more.
Clarifications to my oral remarks
My phrase "7.75 plus interest" did not establish a like-for-like comparison of the scenarios.
They have different capital funding and reserve outcomes. My request remains a comparison of
the full cash and borrowing costs of the same projects.
The historical spending totals I cited combine records at different stages, including forecasts
and proposals, with unresolved differences in accounting scope and treatment. They do not, by
themselves, establish comparable recurring spending, explain the changes between years or
demonstrate management failure. I am not relying on that comparison to support these
requests.
Source note: October 28 Budget-Funding-Scenarios.pdf, physical pages 5, 7 and 9; the $600,000 and $160,000 figures are
from the 2027 PROPOSED columns. This edited version preserves my requests while clarifying the comparisons described
above.
DRAFT

BOROUGH OF WYOMISSING – SPECIAL COUNCIL MINUTES 
NOVEMBER 3, 2026 – 7:00 P.M. 
 
The Borough of Wyomissing Council held their reconvened special budget meeting in the Borough of 
Wyomissing Council Chambers on the above date and time. Council President Kristen M. Gajewski 
called the meeting to order at 7:00 p.m. with the following persons present: Jennifer L. Reimer, Richard 
A. Weinhoffer, Stephen D. Brunner, Jana R. Barnett, Christopher F. Valente, and Adam C. Berkeley 
(7:05 pm); Mayor Frederick C. Levering, Treasurer David Reeser, Public Works Director James Babb, 
Police Chief John Phillips, Fire Chief Eric Needle, Interim Borough Manager Eden Ratliff, Interim 
Chief Financial Officer Scott Boehret, and Borough Secretary Melissa Miller. The following persons 
were absent: Borough Solicitor Heidi Masano and Borough Engineer Jim McCarthy. 
CALL TO ORDER 
 
PLEDGE OF ALLEGIANCE 
ROLL CALL 
VISITORS: 
Tim and Maureen McDonough – 21 Hummingbird Road 
Dan Macrina – 306 Evans Avenue 
Susie Pobsv – 1637 Farr Road 
Mary Jean and Mike Noon – 1637 Farr Road 
Tony Grim – 1148 Old Mill Road 
Sandy Reese – 11 Cardinal Place 
Jim and Karen McAvoy – 1511 Dauphin Avenue

Blake Molinaro – 1546 Dauphin Avenue 
Barbara Reeser – 1110 Fairview Avenue 
Adrian Jadic – 48 Cardinal Road 
Kevin Wolf– 1115 Thrush Road 
Tony Tucci – Western Berks Ambulance Association 
 
The public was reminded that the meeting was a continuation from October 28, 2026, and that no 
opening public comment period would be held. All public comments would be reserved until the 
end public comment period, and the public comment policy was referenced as being on the website 
and posted at Borough Hall.  
 
2027 BUDGET DEVELOPMENT: 
Interim Borough Manager Ratliff shared the 2027 budget preparation schedule with the public. 
He pointed out there was a mistake in the materials provided in preparation for this meeting, 
and a revision to the materials was posted prior to this meeting.   
 
He reminded the public that the 2026 general fund was restructured and since refined with 
DRAFT

overhead transfers realigned, leaving a $2.2 to $2.4 million deficit. While the Borough is not in 
financial crisis, there is concern that more funds are being spent than the Borough’s revenue 
flow. It was again pointed out the staffing related costs encompassing wages, salaries, health, 
pension, payroll tax, other benefits, liability and workers compensation. In many cases, these 
items are established in the collective bargaining agreements, and he noted that annual growth 
is between 4.5% to 5.3%, or $800,000 for 2027.  
 
Interim Manager Ratliff reiterated the five prepared 2027 budget scenarios; raising taxes 7.75 
mils to cover all Borough needs for the year, not implementing a tax increase and downsize 
Borough operations, increase taxes 3 mils to cover operations and fund no capital, increase 
taxes 4 mils taking on some debt and funding capital with less out of reserves, or increase 
taxes 3 mils taking on more debt and funding capital with less out of reserves. 
 
As part of the presentation, Interim Manager Ratliff showed a slide with identified cuts 
including the increase of pool and playground fees by $60,000 and $30,000 respectively, 
eliminate $75,000 from leaf collection, remove Movies in the Park, Memorial Day parade, Egg 
Hunt, Concerts in the Park, Touch A Truck, Ice Skating, and delay the hire of an Accounts 
Receivable Clerk to April 1, 2027. The proposed cuts equate to $251,935. It was pointed out 
that the Recreation Board has administrative oversight over the pool and playground fees, and 
this will be discussed with them, as well as potentially reducing operational costs.  
 
Relative to spending cuts, there was discussion that sponsorships could be obtained for movies 
and concerts, community groups could hold the Memorial Day parade, it was noted the egg 
hunt is a short duration event for the cost, pond preparation for ice skating is almost all over-
time, and the Accounts Receivable position could have a delayed start, with a reminder that the 
position is still vital as there are not enough people to handle funds. Council and the public 
were reminded that the budget is constantly being refined.  
 
Council President Gajewski commented that a tax increase would be needed, but she was not 
comfortable with the level of cuts proposed. She was okay with the increase as long as it was 
known what was being spent where, and the amount cut is not enough to justify giving up on 
the proposed cut items. Mayor Fred Levering was in agreeance, reminding Council and the 
public about history and traditions of the Borough, and what one gets service-wise for their 
dollar. Some of the services provided, are why the residents have chosen to live in 
Wyomissing. He noted that residents decide if they want a unique community, or another place 
to live. He noted the percent of the tax bill that actually gets allocated to the Borough.  
 
Councilmember Berkeley was in agreeance with Mayor Levering in relating to the Borough’s 
offerings being why people live in Wyomissing. He also noted that in realization, once an asset 
is gone, it is not returned. There was also further discussion on the proposed cuts and 
operations.  
 
Councilmember Brunner indicated opposition to cutting the leaf collection service and spoke 
about the $160,000 cost for the communications firm. There was also discussion about the 
Deputy Fire Chief position, pointing out that the position was not in the initial budget, but had 
appeared on the prior presentation as a position for potential hire. There was also concern 
DRAFT

about how the pool and playground fee increases may be necessary, however the Borough 
should be cautious about out rating the program to the point where it becomes economically 
unfeasible to participate. It was pointed out about the interim management fees of $350,000 
and the fact that interim means short-term, not a few years. There was also concern that there 
should be consideration for part-time hires for certain roles, not full-time. It was reiterated 
about the delayed hire for accounts receivable, and the fact that the Zoning/Codes and the 
Permit Coordinator roles would breakeven based on the current amount being spent with the 
third-party codes enforcement. Later in the meeting Councilmember Brunner reiterated the 
prior challenge of tax increase justification if the fund balance was healthy, and identified that 
Council budgeted for general fund deficits in the prior year.  
 
Councilmember Barnett reminded Council and the public that based on a recent municipal 
Manager hire at Cumru township, the salary and benefits equate to $310,000, which is not far 
from the Interim Manager’s costs.  
 
Council President Gajewski indicated that the Borough could pay less for services, however 
Council sought professional Borough Management. Issues have arisen internally and 
budgetarily, and the decision to hire the consulting firm providing the Interim Manager was 
defended. Services rendered now, will help the Borough prepare for permanently hiring a 
Borough Manager.  
 
Councilmember Reimer reminded the public that $350,000 is all encompassing for the Interim 
Manager, with no additional employment-related expenses or costs incurred.  
 
There was public concern that more than 10 people were hired since 2022, and structural 
change is needed. Much concern was raised about decreasing staff, and the number of staff 
cuts needed to balance the budget being 26 people. While there was some community 
opposition of cutting staff, there was more issue with the addition of staff as well as 
implantation of the communications firm.  
 
David Reeser, 1110 Fairview Avenue provided public comment on Police Department and 
Public Works staffing needs. Interim Manager Ratliff shared that more people are needed to do 
the services.  
 
Councilmember Reimer indicated the goal for the pool and playground program fee increase is 
to not necessarily close the gap, but to increase revenue and reduce expenditures.  
 
Councilmember Weinhoffer noted the fee increase discussion could be added to the November 
2026 Recreation Board agenda. He pointed out that the Board and staff worked to decrease 
expenditures for 2026 with several items coming under budget, and pointed out that 
playground and pool fees were increased for 2026. The increases occurred due to them being 
too hard to cut out completely. He expressed that he would not be opposed to consultant cost 
reductions, but reminded Council that they need to work together to dig out slowly.  
 
Councilmember Reimer reiterated that $160,000 for the communications team is equitable to a 
staffer with a $95,000 salary plus employment benefits/costs. With the current 
DRAFT

communications team, the website is updated monthly with information easily shared and 
consumed by the public. It is important to have Kate & Co. for long enough to build the 
platform to allow the next person to “plug and play”. The company provides assistance with 
the website, social media, recreation guide, and media blasts. It was noted that this is not a 
long-term solution, and the company is currently getting info to the public that people want to 
have.  
 
Councilmember Valente noted that several of the current Councilmembers ran on the platform 
of engaging and informing residents, as this was not done previously. The goal was to push 
information to the public so they do not have to attend meetings if they choose not to. He 
noted that he was not fully aware of Wyomissing’s government last year until he started 
attending, and pointed out that the public was only aware of the tax increase in December. He 
is open to hearing the public’s ideas and solving matters together. He reminded the public that 
the use of several of the consultants is temporary for another year, and noted their exceptional 
job and marginal costs. People need to be abreast of information and know where to get it, and 
pointed out the need for professional services. 
 
Councilmember Barnett pointed out that the Borough must work to increase revenue, also 
pointing out the impending Zoning Ordinance to hopefully make the area more attractive to 
developers to eventually help increase economic development and Borough revenues. Interim 
Manager Ratliff shared that during the next Council meeting, three hearings would be held 
pertaining to the items addressed by Councilmember Barnett, and there was also discussion 
about the reduction in real estate taxes at the Berkshire Mall.  
 
Councilmember Brunner is hopeful the overlay will attract robust businesses, but the reality at 
this point is that expenses are rising and revenues are down. He pointed out that the previously 
shown graph does coincide with the Berkshire Mall decline. He also acknowledged the 
increase in health insurance.  
 
Interim Manager Ratliff reviewed the expense drivers. 
 
Councilmember Brunner reiterated that the Borough can only do so much with some of the 
buildings in the commercial district that are economically downtrodden, as they are owner run. 
It was pointed out that it is not just an impact to real estate tax, it is also mercantile, business 
privilege, and real estate transfer taxes.  
 
Based on the identified scenarios, Scenarios 3 and 4 are more viable. Scenario 3 would impose 
a 3 mil tax increase, have a $2.5 million bond, have $410,000 in debt service, and pay for 
some operations out of the existing fund balance. Reserves as a percentage of spending would 
be 12.10% with a general fund balance of $2,157,397.  
 
Scenario 4 would impose a 4 mil tax increase, require less debt, and paying some capital prior 
to bond issuance. If a later project is proposed, some cash can be held for such. This would 
leave 16.40% in reserves and would leave a general fund balance of $2,913,079, and 2027 
surplus of $573,572.  
 
DRAFT

It was pointed out that the tax increase on the average assessed home value is nominal. Further 
discussion ensued about the positions considered for hire since the prior presentation indicated 
5 new hires, and Interim Manager Ratliff reiterated that out of the three positions remaining, 
two are net neutral, and the one will only begin after the first quarter of 2027. There was also 
discussion that two of the proposed positions will generate revenue.  
 
Other discussion ensued pertaining to elimination of the communications team and hiring an 
intern for this role, review of changing insurance brokers, freeze capital expenditures, and 
eliminating new debt service.  
 
Council President Gajewski noted that much capital has already been deferred, and Council 
now has the responsibility to figure out how much revenue needs to increase, minimize 
expenditures, and identify opportunities. She also reiterated the need to have a professional 
manager, communicate with the residents, and pointed out that several engagements were in 
progress without contracts.  
 
Interim Manager Ratliff indicated that without an increase, the Borough would slip to a $3 
million deficit. He also pointed out that several factors fall into increased insurance costs. 
There was public concern that the Borough should reevaluate the insurance broker matter, and 
point made that new things can’t be purchased when there is no money.  
 
Councilmember Brunner explained that changing insurance brokers is different than changing 
the insurance carrier. The broker shops insurance policies to find the best deal. When losses 
are incurred rates increase. Various broker reviews occurred, and upon change of brokers, 
things were found such as properties not insured at proper values, all the while, overall claims 
increased. There was question about whether or not bids occurred, and it was noted that no 
bids occurred, but there were broker interviews. There was also concern about elimination of 
capital but there are certain aspects that cannot be eliminated such as emergency services 
radios, as well as Fire Department air packs.  
 
There was continued public concern about staff cuts, and review of discretionary spending, 
seemingly not being reviewed.  
 
Councilmember Reimer indicated equipment is needed for operations, insurance and CBA 
costs must be taken into consideration, there are few people in the office performing all the 
work, and all the departments the Borough has, are departments providing services that 
residents want.  
 
Councilmember Valente indicated that he trusts the managers when they indicate more people 
are needed and there was brief discussion that cutting people impacts items such as caring for 
the parklands and tending to street trees.  
 
Councilmember Reimer reminded the public that cutting staff would impact street plowing and 
leaf collection, all services the public expects. There was mention that snow removal could be 
reduced if there were no large-scale storms this year as large storms require more equipment 
and manpower to remove excessive snow piles.  
DRAFT

 
Councilmember Brunner reiterated that insurance rates will not be reduced to rates prior due to 
the Borough’s losses.  
 
Council President Gajewski understood the concern of the use of consultants but reiterated that 
the communications firm is not permanent and may not be for the full year. She noted there 
could be more communications pertaining to what all consultants are working on, and a 
majority of Council was in agreeance with maintaining professional services. She was 
favorable of the 4 mil tax increase. The Borough could do 3 mils however feels the Borough 
should be in a position where there is no deficit.  
 
Councilmember Barnett was favorable of the 4 mil increase with management continuing to 
review, refine, and adopt a balanced budget with capital improvements.  
 
Councilmember Reimer spoke about borrowing for capital projects and extending the course 
of life and the time for repayments. She was favorable of a 4 mil increase. 3 mils would be 
difficult and lead to a another increase in 2028, and subsequent years. 
 
Councilmember Weinhoffer was favorable of a maximum 3 mil increase, and potentially 
reducing the Kate & Co. services and eventually transfer away from their services. He 
questioned if there is consideration to reduce their services, why Council would want to budget 
the entire year with them. He was not favorable of a large tax increase for 2027 that all would 
be responsible for, to get the Borough back on track. It was note that with the communications 
aspect, the budget could not be cut for the remainder of the year even if services for Kate & 
Co. are reduced, since someone else would need to manage communications the remainder of 
the year.  
 
Councilmember Barnett made a motion, seconded by Councilmember Berkeley to request 
administrative staff to prepare the draft budget for the November 10, 2026 Council meeting, 
with a 4 mil tax increase, with appropriate allocation to the different tax classification, and 
continue to look for opportunities to cut expenses, motion carries, 4-3. Councilmembers 
Weinhoffer, Brunner, and Valente opposed the motion.  
 
Prior to the finalization of the motion, the upcoming meetings related to budget were 
discussed, as was the discussion pertaining to how to handle the public comment session for 
this topic, as well as the public comment period.  
 
There was also Council discussion about the fire department costing the Borough $3.5 million 
per year to operate, thus was useful to have the fire tax to allocate revenue directly to that 
department. There was brief comment that the state has a cap on the amount of fire tax that can 
be generated.   
 
PUBLIC COMMENT (agenda or non-agenda items): 
Adrian Jadic, 48 Cardinal Road noted that the public should be heard before the vote is taken. 
Later in the public comment period, he provided additional comment pertaining to the 
expenditure increase over several years, documents made available a short time before the 
DRAFT

meeting, lack of RFPs, the high tax levy per person, and the value of 1 mil in Wyomissing 
versus other municipalities.  
 
Tim McDonough, 21 Humming Bird Road provided public comment first thanking the 
consultants for their work, and further pertaining to contract lengths of consultants, the review 
of staff needs and budgetary line items, and Borough vacant lots that could be sold.  
 
Blake Molinaro, 1546 Dauphin Avenue provided public comment on the proposed list of 
spending cuts, the use of specified tax funds, and comment on the 2025 Council election. 
 
David Reeser, 1110 Fairview Avenue provided public comment on the communications team, 
and proposed list of spending cuts.  
 
Jim McAvoy, 1511 Dauphin Avenue provided public comment on the Borough’s historical 
roots, and common sense budgeting and fiscal responsibility.  
 
Brock Molinaro, 1713 Meadowlark Road provided public comment on the consultant firms, 
not cutting essential services, spending cuts, fiscal responsibility, and the Borough-wide 
insurance.  
 
Dan Macrina, 306 Evans Avenue provided public comment on prior Borough spending, his 
working history with the Borough and challenges with budget cuts and continuing services, his 
support for Council, and support of a 4 mil increase.  
 
Kevin Wolf, 1115 Thrush Road provided public comment on the increased cost of living in 
Wyomissing over the last several years with no change or benefit, the proposed list of 
spending, seeking RFPs to help cut costs, and reviewing other costs to cut. 
 
COUNCIL COMMENT: 
Council President Gajewski noted that when sitting in meetings prior to the election, decisions 
were made that couldn’t be reconciled. While on Council, it has been eye-opening to 
understand the state the Borough is in. It is deterring to see a tax increase with no service 
additions or improvements, but it will put the Borough in a position where it is steady. She 
commented on the capacity to do bids and RFPs, and reiterated that staff are overworked, 
that’s why certain pieces such as purchase orders and RFPs are challenging.  
 
Councilmember Valente thanked the public for attending the meeting and providing input; this 
will help Council make informed decisions. He used the analogy that the Borough is sick and 
needs to get healthy. The Borough will be okay at 3 mils, but 4 mils is healthier. He pointed 
out the sacrifices and budgetary cuts being proposed, and acknowledged the public’s ideas of 
ways to raise money. There are many ways to solve the issue and there is room for creativity to 
do so. He emphasized the investment in the Borough and that management should not change 
now, reiterating the value of service with the consultants as compared to the trash hauler.   
 
 
The Wyomissing Borough Council meeting adjourned at 9:14 p.m.

 Kristen M. Gajewski
